# Item

## id
CON-NEU-560394DA6EBE32

## label
The tectum is formed of four colliculi (two superior, two inferior)

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.midbrain.tectum-formed-of-four-colliculi

## definition
The tectum, the roof (dorsal part) of the midbrain, is formed by the corpora quadrigemina: two superior colliculi (visual reflex centers) and two inferior colliculi (auditory relay centers) — four colliculi in total, distinguishing the tectum from the tegmentum and crus cerebri.

## explicit_objective
State that the midbrain tectum is formed of the four colliculi (corpora quadrigemina), not geniculate bodies, cochlear nuclei or salivatory nuclei.

definition

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4EA5872F5CE302

## label
The internal jugular vein (jugular bulb) is related to the floor of the middle ear

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.internal-jugular-vein.floor-of-middle-ear-relation

## definition
The jugular bulb, the dilated superior end of the internal jugular vein, sits within the jugular fossa of the temporal bone immediately below the floor of the middle ear (tympanic cavity), separated from it by a thin, sometimes dehiscent, bony plate.

## explicit_objective
State that the internal jugular vein (via the jugular bulb) is related to the floor of the middle ear, not its roof, posterior or anterior wall.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-86E4A77B496E9F

## label
Reciprocal innervation relaxes the antagonist muscle during the stretch reflex

## canonical_key
physiology.stretch-reflex.reciprocal-innervation-relaxes-antagonist

## definition
In reciprocal innervation, the Ia afferent that excites a stretched muscle's own alpha motor neurons monosynaptically also excites a spinal inhibitory interneuron that inhibits the antagonist muscle's motor neurons, so the antagonist relaxes as the agonist contracts.

## explicit_objective
State that antagonist muscle relaxation during the stretch reflex is produced by reciprocal innervation (Ia afferent collaterals inhibiting antagonist motor neurons via an interneuron).

mechanism

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-81FB852F568E04

## label
The anterior nucleus of the thalamus is the thalamic relay of the Papez circuit for emotion and memory

## canonical_key
physiology.limbic-system.anterior-thalamic-nucleus-emotion-memory

## definition
The anterior nucleus of the thalamus is a relay of the Papez circuit (hippocampus, via the fornix, to the mammillary bodies, via the mammillothalamic tract to the anterior thalamic nucleus, to the cingulate gyrus, and back to the hippocampus), the classical anatomical substrate for emotion and memory processing.

## explicit_objective
Identify the anterior nucleus of the thalamus as the thalamic relay of the Papez circuit for emotion and memory processing.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-AA621897598B60

## label
The cupula of the crista ampullaris is a gelatinous layer of proteoglycans

## canonical_key
histology.crista-ampullaris.cupula-gelatinous-proteoglycan-layer

## definition
The cupula is a dome-shaped, gelatinous mass composed largely of proteoglycans that caps each crista ampullaris and embeds the stereocilia and kinocilium of the underlying hair cells, so that endolymph flow from angular acceleration deflects it and bends the embedded cilia.

## explicit_objective
State that the cupula, a gelatinous proteoglycan mass, forms the structure of the crista ampullaris that embeds the hair cells' stereocilia and kinocilium.

## label
The caudate nucleus and putamen are basal ganglia nuclei

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.basal-ganglia.caudate-putamen-are-basal-ganglia-nuclei

## definition
The caudate nucleus and putamen together form the corpus striatum (neostriatum), a core component of the basal ganglia alongside the globus pallidus, subthalamic nucleus and substantia nigra.

## explicit_objective
Recognize the caudate nucleus and putamen as genuine basal ganglia nuclei, distinguishing them from mammillary bodies, gracile/cuneate nuclei and the inferior olive.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-6C66C7A9941709

## label
The central sulcus separates the frontal lobe from the parietal lobe

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.cerebral-cortex.central-sulcus-separates-frontal-parietal-lobes

## definition
The central sulcus (of Rolando) runs obliquely from the superomedial border of the hemisphere down toward the lateral sulcus, separating the precentral gyrus (frontal lobe, motor cortex) from the postcentral gyrus (parietal lobe, sensory cortex) — the anatomical boundary between the frontal and parietal lobes.

## explicit_objective
State that the central sulcus separates the frontal lobe from the parietal lobe.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-8573B7146A58C5

## label
The anterior corneal surface provides about two-thirds of the resting eye's refractive power

## canonical_key
physiology.eye-optics.cornea-anterior-surface-two-thirds-refractive-power

## definition
Of the resting eye's total refractive power of roughly 59-60 diopters, the anterior corneal surface alone contributes about two-thirds (around 40-43 diopters), since it is where the greatest refractive-index change occurs (air to cornea); the crystalline lens contributes only the remaining third.

## explicit_objective
State that the anterior surface of the cornea provides about two-thirds of the resting eye's total refractive power.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-B74B01E1CC0BC9

## label
The tectorial membrane is a gelatinous mass of collagen fibrils

## canonical_key
histology.organ-of-corti.tectorial-membrane-gelatinous-collagen

## definition
The tectorial membrane is a gelatinous, largely acellular structure made of collagen fibrils in a glycoprotein matrix, overlying the organ of Corti within the scala media, against which the outer hair cells' stereocilia shear when the basilar membrane vibrates.

## explicit_objective
State that the tectorial membrane, a gelatinous mass of collagen fibrils, is the structure of the scala media overlying the organ of Corti.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-54D3211A36271C

## label
Spinal shock is characterized acutely by loss of spinal reflexes

## canonical_key
physiology.spinal-shock.characterized-by-loss-of-spinal-reflexes

## definition
Spinal shock is the acute, transient state immediately following severe spinal cord injury, characterized by complete loss (areflexia) of all reflex activity below the level of the lesion, with flaccid paralysis and hypotonia, from sudden withdrawal of descending facilitatory input; hyperreflexia and spasticity emerge only later, once spinal shock resolves.

## explicit_objective
State that spinal shock is characterized acutely by loss of spinal reflexes below the level of the lesion, distinct from the chronic hyperreflexia/spasticity that follow once it resolves.

The cerebellum's function is purely motor control at a subconscious level

## canonical_key

## definition

# Item

## id
CON-FND-4FA204727104EB

## label
Unilateral cleft lip results from failure of fusion between the maxillary process and the medial nasal process

## canonical_key
embryology.face-development.accutane-unilateral-cleft-lip-maxillary-medial-nasal-fusion

## definition
The upper lip forms from fusion of the two medial nasal processes in the midline with the maxillary process on each side; failure of fusion between a maxillary process and the medial nasal process on one side produces a unilateral cleft lip, the most common form of cleft lip.

## explicit_objective
Identify failure of fusion between the maxillary process and medial nasal process as the cause of unilateral cleft lip, distinguishing it from median cleft lip and oblique facial cleft mechanisms.

A 17-year-old girl uses Accutane, an acne drug implicated in interfering with normal development of the facial primordia, early in her unexpected pregnancy. Examination of the newborn reveals the presence of unilateral cleft upper lip. In this case, the defect is related to failure of fusion of what craniofacial processes? A. Maxillary and lateral nasal processes B. Medial and lateral nasal processes C. Maxillary and medial nasal processes D. Opposite maxillary processes Key: C.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-F285418BE7B987

## label
Efferent (motor) neurons of the spinal cord are located in the ventral and lateral grey horns

## canonical_key
physiology.spinal-cord.efferent-motor-neurons-ventral-lateral-horns

## definition
Alpha and gamma motor neurons (lower motor neurons supplying skeletal muscle) have their cell bodies in the ventral (anterior) grey horn of the spinal cord, while sympathetic preganglionic efferent neurons lie in the lateral grey horn at thoracolumbar levels.

## explicit_objective
State that efferent (motor) neurons of the spinal cord are located in the ventral and lateral grey horns, not in white matter columns or the dorsal horn.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-90460E733F4EBC

## label
Destruction of the lateral hypothalamic nucleus causes anorexia

## canonical_key
physiology.hypothalamus.lateral-nucleus-destruction-causes-anorexia

## definition
The lateral hypothalamic nuclei constitute the "feeding" or "hunger center"; their destruction abolishes the desire to eat, producing anorexia and severe weight loss, while their stimulation induces feeding — the opposite role to the ventromedial nucleus ("satiety center"), whose destruction causes hyperphagia.

## explicit_objective
State that destruction of the lateral hypothalamic nuclei (the feeding center) causes anorexia, contrasting this with the ventromedial nucleus's opposite (satiety) role.

Each vertebral artery ascends through the foramina transversaria of the upper cervical vertebrae, winds around the lateral mass of the atlas, pierces the posterior atlanto-occipital membrane and dura, and enters the cranial cavity through the foramen magnum.

## label
Pill-rolling tremor is a separate Parkinson disease feature, not a sequela of rigidity

## canonical_key
physiology.parkinsonism.rigidity-sequelae-excludes-tremor

## definition
Pill-rolling (resting) tremor is a separate cardinal feature of Parkinson disease, arising from an oscillating basal ganglia circuit, distinct from the sequelae that are directly produced by rigidity itself (bradykinesia/akinesia, mask facies, loss of arm swing during gait).

## explicit_objective
Identify pill-rolling tremor as a separate cardinal feature of Parkinson disease, distinct from the sequelae directly produced by rigidity (bradykinesia, mask face, loss of arm swing).

## label
The cognitive function of the basal ganglia relates to its connection with the prefrontal cortex

## canonical_key
physiology.basal-ganglia.cognitive-function-prefrontal-cortex-connection

## definition
The associative (cognitive) basal ganglia circuit runs chiefly through the caudate nucleus, which receives projections from, and returns output via the thalamus to, the prefrontal cortex, underlying executive function and the cognitive aspects of basal ganglia activity, distinct from the motor (putamen-based) and limbic (ventral-striatum-based) circuits.

## explicit_objective

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-F12F9A014A1F83

## label
The tympanic branch belongs to the glossopharyngeal nerve, not the vagus nerve

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.vagus-nerve.branches-exclude-tympanic

## definition
The vagus nerve gives auricular (Arnold's nerve), pharyngeal and meningeal (recurrent) branches, but the tympanic branch instead belongs to the glossopharyngeal nerve (Jacobson's nerve), supplying the tympanic plexus of the middle ear.

## explicit_objective
Identify the tympanic branch as belonging to the glossopharyngeal nerve, not the vagus nerve, distinguishing it from the vagus's genuine auricular, pharyngeal and meningeal branches.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-1A9C20FE7E4575

## label
Betz cells are giant pyramidal neurons in the inner pyramidal layer of the motor cortex

## canonical_key
histology.motor-cortex.betz-cells-inner-pyramidal-layer

## definition
Betz cells are giant pyramidal neurons in layer V (the internal/inner pyramidal layer) of the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus); their large-diameter, heavily myelinated axons contribute to the corticospinal (pyramidal) tract, making them upper, not lower, motor neurons.

## explicit_objective
State that Betz cells are giant upper motor neurons found in the inner pyramidal layer (layer V) of the precentral gyrus.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E698018A0AF277

## label
The fovea centralis is the region of highest visual acuity in the retina

## canonical_key
physiology.retina.fovea-centralis-highest-visual-acuity

## definition
The fovea centralis, at the center of the macula, is densely packed exclusively with cone photoreceptors and has minimal neural convergence, giving it the sharpest spatial resolution and the highest visual acuity in the retina; it has the highest, not the lowest, light threshold and is specialized for photopic rather than scotopic vision.

## explicit_objective
State that the fovea centralis is the region of highest visual acuity in the retina, being cone-only and specialized for photopic (not scotopic) vision.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-AD1CE5E574BD57

## label
Contralateral extensor hypertonia is not a result of semicircular canal stimulation

## canonical_key
physiology.semicircular-canals.stimulation-results-exclude-contralateral-extensor-hypertonia

## definition
Semicircular canal (rotational) stimulation classically produces nystagmus, vertigo and vomiting; contralateral extensor hypertonia is instead associated with static (otolith/utricular) labyrinthine reflexes regulating postural tone via the vestibulospinal tract, not a direct semicircular-canal effect.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E7BE6F1F77A147

## label
In the horizontal semicircular canal, the kinocilium lies toward the utricle

## canonical_key
physiology.crista-ampullaris.horizontal-canal-kinocilium-toward-utricle

## definition
In the horizontal (lateral) semicircular canal, each hair cell's single kinocilium is positioned on the utricular side of the stereocilia bundle, so that endolymph flow deflecting the stereocilia toward the kinocilium (utriculopetal flow) depolarizes the cell — the opposite polarity arrangement from the vertical canals.

## explicit_objective
State that in the horizontal semicircular canal, the kinocilium of each crista ampullaris hair cell lies toward the utricle, so that utriculopetal endolymph flow is depolarizing.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4A995422A92889

## label
Zonular fibers are not a component of the eyelid

## canonical_key
histology.eyelid.contents-exclude-zonule-fibers

## definition
The eyelid's structural framework includes the tarsal plate, smooth muscle (such as the tarsal muscles of Müller) and a rich blood supply, but not zonular fibers, which belong instead to the lens/ciliary apparatus (the suspensory ligament of the lens) deep within the globe.

## explicit_objective
Identify zonular fibers as belonging to the lens/ciliary apparatus, not the eyelid, distinguishing them from genuine eyelid components (tarsal plate, smooth muscle, blood vessels).

# Item

## id
CON-FND-7602CDE0FF0632

## label
The fourth ventricle is the derivative of the hindbrain's central cavity

## canonical_key
embryology.ventricular-system.fourth-ventricle-from-hindbrain-cavity

## definition
During neural tube development, the central cavity of the rhombencephalon (hindbrain) expands into the fourth ventricle, situated between the pons/medulla ventrally and the cerebellum dorsally, distinct from the lateral ventricles (telencephalon), third ventricle (diencephalon) and central canal (spinal cord) derivatives.

## explicit_objective
State that the fourth ventricle is the derivative of the hindbrain's central cavity, distinguishing it from the lateral ventricles, third ventricle and central canal.

## id

## label
The medial mammillary nucleus lies in the posterior (mammillary) zone of the hypothalamus

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.hypothalamus.nuclei-zones-medial-mammillary-posterior

## definition
The hypothalamus is classically divided anteroposteriorly into supraoptic (anterior), tuberal (middle/intermediate) and mammillary (posterior) zones; the medial mammillary nucleus, part of the mammillary body complex, lies within the posterior (mammillary) zone.

## explicit_objective
State that the medial mammillary nucleus lies in the posterior (mammillary) zone of the hypothalamus, per its anteroposterior zonal organization.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-FE11408FE89F6F

## label
The chorda tympani carries taste, not general sensation, from the anterior two-thirds of the tongue

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.chorda-tympani.does-not-carry-general-sensation-anterior-tongue

## definition
The chorda tympani, a facial nerve branch crossing the middle ear cavity, carries taste sensation only from the anterior two-thirds of the tongue (general sensation there is carried separately by the lingual nerve) plus preganglionic parasympathetic secretomotor fibers, via the submandibular ganglion, to the submandibular and sublingual glands.

## explicit_objective
Identify that the chorda tympani carries taste, not general sensation, from the anterior two-thirds of the tongue, while correctly carrying secretomotor fibers to the submandibular and sublingual glands.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-A9BA2DDBAAA10A

## label
The brain's energy source in the fed state is glucose

## canonical_key
physiology.brain-metabolism.fed-state-energy-from-glucose

## definition
In the fed (well-nourished) state, the brain relies almost exclusively on glucose as its energy substrate, since glucose crosses the blood-brain barrier efficiently while neurons have very limited capacity to oxidize fatty acids; ketone bodies become a significant alternative fuel only during prolonged fasting.

## explicit_objective
State that in the fed state, the brain's energy source is glucose, contrasting this with the fasting state's reliance on ketone bodies.

The dentate nucleus is a cerebellar, not basal ganglia, nucleus

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-138F33804B1E30

## label
The anterior cerebral artery courses within the callosal sulcus

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.anterior-cerebral-artery.lies-in-callosal-sulcus

## definition
After curving around the genu of the corpus callosum, the anterior cerebral artery continues posteriorly as the pericallosal artery within the callosal sulcus, immediately above the corpus callosum, supplying the medial surface of the frontal and parietal lobes.

## explicit_objective
State that the anterior cerebral artery (as the pericallosal artery) courses within the callosal sulcus.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-815C00BF788641

## label
The body of the caudate nucleus relates to the central part of the lateral ventricle

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.caudate-nucleus.body-relates-to-central-part-lateral-ventricle

## definition
The C-shaped caudate nucleus follows the curve of the lateral ventricle: its head bulges into the anterior horn, its body runs along the floor of the central part (body) of the lateral ventricle, and its tail curves through the roof of the inferior horn down to the amygdala.

## explicit_objective
State that the body of the caudate nucleus is related to the central part of the lateral ventricle, distinguishing this from the head (anterior horn) and tail (inferior horn) relations.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-7057F43372D93C

## label
Post-tetanic potentiation increases neurotransmitter release via presynaptic residual calcium

## canonical_key
physiology.synaptic-transmission.post-tetanic-potentiation-mechanism

## definition
Brief high-frequency (tetanic) presynaptic stimulation causes residual calcium to accumulate in the presynaptic terminal faster than it can be cleared, so a subsequent test stimulus triggers exaggerated neurotransmitter release — post-tetanic potentiation, a simple cellular model of short-term synaptic plasticity.

## explicit_objective
State that brief post-tetanic stimulation causes increased neurotransmitter release and synaptic potentiation, via presynaptic residual calcium accumulation.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-2599FCF96871C9

## label
The cerebellothalamocortical pathway is the ascending efferent (output) route from the deep cerebellar nuclei to the motor cortex

## canonical_key
cerebellum.efferent.cerebellothalamocortical-tract

## definition
Efferent fibers from the deep cerebellar nuclei (chiefly dentate) cross in the superior cerebellar peduncle, relay in the contralateral red nucleus and ventral lateral thalamus, and ascend to the motor cortex — the cerebellothalamocortical pathway.

## explicit_objective
State that the cerebellothalamocortical pathway is an ascending efferent route from the cerebellum, distinguishing it from the afferent spinocerebellar and descending cerebello-reticular/olivary pathways.

The lens and its fibers are enveloped by the lens capsule

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-B2826E3593B451

## label
Purkinje cell bodies form a single row at the molecular-granular layer junction, not aggregated, stellate, or superficial in the molecular layer

## canonical_key
cerebellum.histology.purkinje-cell-none-of-the-above-distractor-set

## definition
Purkinje cells are large, flask-shaped neurons whose cell bodies form a single row at the junction between the cerebellar cortex's molecular and granular layers, with a dendritic tree arborizing into the molecular layer — distinct from the densely aggregated granule cells or the superficial molecular-layer stellate cells.

## explicit_objective
Recognize that Purkinje cells sit at the molecular-granular layer junction as large solitary flask-shaped neurons, distinguishing them from granule cells and stellate cells.

## id
CON-NEU-BE8AFA373D630C

## label
Visceral pain afferents travel with sympathetic nerves in the thorax and abdomen

## canonical_key
pain.physiology.visceral-pain-afferents-travel-with-sympathetic-nerves

## definition
Visceral pain (nociceptive) afferent fibers from thoracic and abdominal organs travel back to the spinal cord alongside sympathetic efferent nerves, entering at multiple segmental levels and converging on dorsal horn neurons shared with somatic afferents — the anatomical basis of referred pain.

## explicit_objective
State that visceral pain signals are transmitted along sensory fibers that travel with sympathetic nerves in the abdomen and thorax.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-92A815DDD5B14F

## label
The Golgi tendon reflex is an inhibitory, disynaptic spinal reflex

## canonical_key
reflex.physiology.golgi-tendon-reflex-inhibitory-disynaptic

## definition
The Golgi tendon (Ib) reflex is disynaptic and inhibitory: Ib afferents excite one spinal inhibitory interneuron, which inhibits alpha motor neurons of the same muscle (autogenic inhibition) — unlike the monosynaptic excitatory stretch reflex or the polysynaptic crossed extensor/flexion withdrawal reflexes.

## explicit_objective
Identify the Golgi tendon reflex as the inhibitory, disynaptic spinal reflex, distinguishing it from the excitatory stretch reflex and the polysynaptic crossed extensor and flexion withdrawal reflexes.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-35A39A6816EDDC

## label
This source credits the lateral reticulospinal tract with inhibiting skeletal muscle tone contralaterally

## canonical_key
brainstem.physiology.lateral-reticulospinal-tract-source-credits-contralateral-inhibition

## definition
The lateral (medullary) reticulospinal tract, arising from the medullary reticular formation, inhibits skeletal muscle tone. This source's printed key credits that inhibitory action as contralateral, though mainstream teaching more commonly describes the tract's descent and action as predominantly ipsilateral — recorded as printed, with the conflict noted here rather than silently corrected.

## explicit_objective
State this source's credited function of the lateral reticulospinal tract (inhibits skeletal muscle tone contralaterally), noting the conflict with the more commonly taught ipsilateral-dominant pattern.

definition

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E8E2F338F0686F

## label
A positive Babinski sign is a hallmark feature of an upper motor neuron lesion

## canonical_key
spinalcord.physiology.upper-motor-neuron-lesion-positive-babinski-sign

## definition
An upper motor neuron lesion classically produces a positive (extensor, upgoing) Babinski sign — dorsiflexion of the great toe with fanning of the other toes on plantar stimulation — reflecting loss of descending corticospinal inhibition, distinct from the remarkable wasting of a lower motor neuron lesion.

## explicit_objective
State that an upper motor neuron lesion is associated with a positive Babinski sign.

Agraphia is caused by a lesion of the hand skills area

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-A4C9EDFB508641

## label
The spinothalamic tract crosses within a segment or two of its level of spinal cord entry

## canonical_key
spinalcord.anatomy.spinothalamic-tract-crosses-at-level-of-entry

## definition
Spinothalamic tract second-order neurons cross to the contralateral side via the anterior white commissure within one to two segments of their level of spinal entry, unlike the uncrossed dorsal spinocerebellar tract, the doubly-crossing ventral spinocerebellar tract, or the medullary-crossing medial lemniscus.

## explicit_objective
State that the spinothalamic tract crosses at the spinal cord level of entry, distinguishing it from tracts that cross elsewhere or not at all.

The rebound phenomenon in cerebellar disease reflects loss of the damping function

## canonical_key

## label
The superior temporal gyrus (auditory cortex) is not part of the rotatory nystagmus pathway

## canonical_key
vestibular.physiology.rotatory-nystagmus-pathway-excludes-superior-temporal-gyrus

## definition
The rotatory nystagmus pathway runs through the vestibular nucleus, medial longitudinal fasciculus and reticular formation to the extraocular motor nuclei; the superior temporal gyrus (auditory cortex) is not part of this vestibulo-ocular circuit.

## explicit_objective
Identify the superior temporal gyrus as excluded from the rotatory nystagmus neural pathway, unlike the medial longitudinal fasciculus, reticular formation and vestibular nucleus.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-175E84DDFEDEA1

## label
Rhodopsin activation by light decreases intracellular cGMP, closing cation channels and hyperpolarizing the photoreceptor

## canonical_key
retina.physiology.rhodopsin-activation-decreases-cgmp-hyperpolarizes

## definition
Light-activated rhodopsin activates transducin, which activates phosphodiesterase to hydrolyze cGMP; the resulting fall in cGMP closes cGMP-gated Na+ channels, reducing the dark current and hyperpolarizing (not depolarizing) the photoreceptor.

## explicit_objective
State that activation of rhodopsin by light energy results in a decreased level of cGMP.

## label
Macula sacculi hair cell stereocilia are embedded into the otolithic membrane

## canonical_key
vestibular.histology.macula-sacculi-type2-hair-cells-stereocilia-otolithic-membrane

## definition
In the macula sacculi, hair cell stereocilia (with a kinocilium) are embedded into the overlying otolithic membrane, a gelatinous, otoconia-studded structure — distinct from the cochlea's tectorial membrane and the crista ampullaris's cupula.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-26CAD5F218C702

## label
High astrocytic NH4+ raises glutamine, drawing water in by osmosis (astrocyte swelling)

## canonical_key
astrocyte.physiology.high-ammonia-increases-glutamine-triggers-water-uptake

## definition
Astrocytes detoxify excess ammonia via glutamine synthetase (glutamate + NH3 → glutamine); the resulting glutamine accumulation raises intracellular osmotic pressure, drawing water in and contributing to astrocyte swelling, as in hepatic encephalopathy/hyperammonemia.

## explicit_objective
State that high astrocytic NH4+ leads to increased glutamine, which triggers water uptake into the astrocyte.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-24787DF6551D8A

## label
GABA is the major inhibitory neurotransmitter of the central, not peripheral, nervous system

## canonical_key
gaba.physiology.most-abundant-in-cns-not-pns

## definition
GABA is the major inhibitory neurotransmitter predominantly of the central nervous system (brain and spinal cord), not a transmitter that is most abundant in the peripheral nervous system.

## explicit_objective
Identify that GABA being most abundant in the peripheral nervous system is false, since it is chiefly the major inhibitory transmitter of the central nervous system.

## label
The cuneate tract is absent at lumbar spinal cord levels

## canonical_key
spinalcord.anatomy.lumbar-segment-cuneate-tract-absent

The cuneate fasciculus, carrying fine touch/proprioception from the upper limb and upper trunk (above roughly T6), is absent at lumbar spinal cord levels, where only the gracile fasciculus is present in the dorsal columns.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E6519393BD5C33

## label
In the lower neck, the carotid sheath contains the common, not internal, carotid artery

## canonical_key
neck.anatomy.carotid-sheath-lower-neck-common-not-internal-carotid

## definition
In the lower part of the neck, the carotid sheath's arterial content is the common carotid artery; the internal carotid artery is present only above the carotid bifurcation (upper neck), alongside the vagus nerve and internal jugular vein, which run the sheath's full length.

## explicit_objective
Identify that the internal carotid artery, unlike the common carotid artery, vagus nerve and internal jugular vein, is not a content of the carotid sheath in the lower part of the neck.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-2FE85C7DF07764

## label
Dorsal column second-order neurons cross at the level of the medulla

## canonical_key
spinalcord.anatomy.dorsal-column-second-order-neurons-cross-at-medulla

## definition
Dorsal column primary afferents ascend uncrossed to synapse in the gracile and cuneate nuclei of the medulla; these second-order neurons then cross as internal arcuate fibers to form the contralateral medial lemniscus — the dorsal column system's crossing point.

## explicit_objective
State that crossing of the dorsal column system's second-order neurons occurs at the level of the medulla.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-0A86B676A56613

## label
Brown-Séquard syndrome causes ipsilateral loss of vibration sense below the lesion

## canonical_key
spinalcord.pathology.brown-sequard-syndrome-ipsilateral-vs-contralateral-loss

## definition
Brown-Séquard syndrome (spinal cord hemisection) produces ipsilateral loss of vibration/fine touch/proprioception (uncrossed dorsal column) and ipsilateral motor loss (already-crossed corticospinal tract) below the lesion, together with contralateral loss of pain/temperature (spinothalamic tract, crossed near its level of entry).

## explicit_objective
State that Brown-Séquard syndrome is characterized by loss of vibration sense on the same side below the level of the lesion.

definition

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-7AF1A6DC04D950

## label
The first synapse for peripheral sensation is on posterior (dorsal) horn cells

## canonical_key
sensory.physiology.first-synapse-for-peripheral-sensation-posterior-horn-cells

## definition
Primary sensory afferent fibers entering the spinal cord typically make their first synapse on second-order neurons in the posterior (dorsal) horn of the spinal cord grey matter.

## explicit_objective
State that the first synapse for peripheral sensation is on posterior horn cells.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-658B16E869AB30

## label
This source credits stimulation of the medial right primary motor area with moving the left hand, though standard homunculus teaching predicts the left foot

## canonical_key
cerebralcortex.homunculus.medial-primary-motor-area-stimulation-produces-contralateral-foot-movement

## definition
This source's printed key credits stimulation of the medial part of the right primary motor area with producing left hand movement. Standard motor homunculus teaching instead places the lower limb (leg/foot), not the hand, on the medial cortical surface, so contralateral (left) FOOT movement would be the physiologically expected answer — recorded here as a printed-key conflict rather than silently corrected.

## explicit_objective
State this source's credited effect of stimulating the medial part of the right primary motor area, noting the conflict with standard motor homunculus topography (which would predict foot, not hand, movement).

## concept_type
definition

## approved_video_resource_ids

## conflicts
Standard motor homunculus teaching represents the lower limb (leg/foot) on the medial surface of the primary motor cortex and the hand laterally; stimulating the medial part of the right motor cortex would therefore be expected to produce contralateral (left) FOOT movement, not left hand movement as this source's printed key credits.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-30EE29AF310C72

## label
The pons develops from the metencephalon, the same hindbrain vesicle that forms the cerebellum

## canonical_key
pons.embryological-origin.metencephalon

## definition
The rhombencephalon (hindbrain) divides into a rostral metencephalon and a caudal myelencephalon. The metencephalon gives rise to two structures on either side of the fourth ventricle: the pons ventrally (basilar pons) and the cerebellum dorsally, while the myelencephalon forms the medulla oblongata caudal to the pons.

## explicit_objective
State that the pons develops from the metencephalon, the same hindbrain vesicle that gives rise to the cerebellum.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-2E874819766DF9

## label
The ventral visual stream's secondary areas interpret fine object detail and color

## canonical_key
visualcortex.ventralstream.function

## definition
The visual association cortex splits into two functionally distinct streams beyond the primary visual cortex: the dorsal ('where/how') stream to the posterior parietal lobe, specialised for spatial localisation and visually guided motor action including motion sensitivity, and the ventral ('what') stream to the inferior temporal lobe, specialised for interpreting fine object detail, shape and color to support object identification and recognition.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish the ventral visual stream's object-identification function (fine detail, color) from the dorsal stream's spatial/motion function.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-3DFC4D9B9DDB66

## label
The fastigial nucleus is the deep cerebellar nucleus concerned with equilibrium

## canonical_key
cerebellarnuclei.fastigial.equilibrium

## definition
The cerebellum has three functional zones, each projecting to a corresponding deep cerebellar nucleus: the medial vestibulocerebellum/vermis projects to the fastigial nucleus (equilibrium, axial tone), the intermediate spinocerebellum projects to the interposed (globose + emboliform) nuclei (limb coordination), and the lateral cerebrocerebellum projects to the dentate nucleus (movement planning).

## explicit_objective
Identify the fastigial nucleus as the deep cerebellar nucleus concerned with equilibrium, distinct from the dentate and interposed nuclei.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-32DC1D1BC605B5

## label
Facial 'danger triangle' infections spread to the cavernous sinus via the valveless ophthalmic vein

## canonical_key
cavernoussinus.thrombophlebitis.ophthalmicvein-route

## definition
The face's 'danger triangle' (bridge of the nose to the corners of the mouth) drains via the facial and angular veins, which anastomose directly and without valves with the superior ophthalmic vein, which in turn drains into the cavernous sinus. This valveless connection allows infection at the nasal tip or upper lip to spread retrograde into the cavernous sinus, producing cavernous sinus thrombophlebitis with periorbital edema, ophthalmoplegia (cranial nerves III, IV, VI in its lateral wall) and fever.

## explicit_objective
Explain why an infection in the facial 'danger triangle' spreads to the cavernous sinus via the valveless ophthalmic vein.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E5D792C3E20EC2

## label
This source credits the frontal eye field (area 8) as the corticonuclear tract's main cortical origin

## canonical_key
corticonucleartract.origin.primarymotorarea4

## definition
This source's printed key credits the frontal eye field (Brodmann area 8) as the main area of origin of the corticonuclear tract, reflecting area 8's role in generating the tract's fibres for voluntary conjugate eye movement via the oculomotor nuclei. Most general descriptions instead credit the primary motor cortex (area 4), with premotor (area 6) contribution, as the tract's main origin for cranial nerve motor nuclei broadly — recorded here as a printed-key conflict rather than silently corrected.

## explicit_objective
State this source's credited cortical area of origin for the corticonuclear tract, noting the conflict with the more commonly taught primary-motor-cortex origin.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids

## topic
Descending motor tracts

## subtopic
Corticonuclear (corticobulbar) tract origin (printed-key doubt)

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls

## article_ids
ART-NEU-AU-MED-203-QUIZ1-ANATOMY

## related_article_ids
ART-NEU-AU-MED-203-QUIZ1-ANATOMY

## related_concept_ids

## approved_file_resource_ids

## approved_video_resource_ids

## conflicts
Most standard teaching describes the corticonuclear (corticobulbar) tract's main origin as the primary motor cortex (area 4), together with premotor contributions (area 6), for cranial nerve motor nuclei generally — not area 8 specifically. This source's printed key instead credits the frontal eye field (area 8), which is more specifically the origin of the tract's fibres controlling voluntary conjugate eye movements via the oculomotor nuclei, a narrower and less commonly emphasised sub-component of the corticonuclear system. Recorded as printed rather than silently corrected to area 4.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-BA1632D7EF23C1

## label
The spinothalamic tract carries slow pain and temperature sensation

## canonical_key
spinothalamictract.slowpaintemperature

## definition
The (lateral) spinothalamic tract carries pain and temperature sensation from the contralateral body to the thalamus, ascending in the anterolateral white matter of the spinal cord after its second-order neuron decussates near the level of entry. Slow, dull, poorly-localised (second) pain, carried by unmyelinated C fibres, travels via this same anterolateral system through multiple relays (reticular formation, intralaminar thalamic nuclei), distinguishing it from the fine touch/proprioception carried by the dorsal columns.

## explicit_objective
Identify the spinothalamic tract as the pathway carrying slow pain and temperature sensation.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-62E82BB2967D13

## label
This source excludes the spinal cord from the vestibular nuclei's efferent projections

## canonical_key
vestibularnuclei.efferentprojections

## definition
This source's printed key states that the vestibular nuclei do NOT send efferents to the spinal cord, conflicting with the well-established vestibulospinal tracts (lateral and medial), which carry vestibular nuclear efferents to the spinal cord to control extensor (antigravity) muscle tone and head/neck posture. Standard teaching instead lists the cerebellum, reticular formation, spinal cord (vestibulospinal tracts), and oculomotor nuclei (via the medial longitudinal fasciculus) among the vestibular nuclei's efferent targets, with the basal ganglia not among their classically described direct projections.

## explicit_objective
State this source's printed exception among the vestibular nuclei's efferent projections, noting the conflict with the well-established vestibulospinal tract to the spinal cord.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids

## topic
Vestibular system

## subtopic
Vestibular nuclear efferent projections (printed-key doubt)

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## related_article_ids
ART-NEU-AU-MED-203-QUIZ1-ANATOMY

## approved_file_resource_ids

## approved_video_resource_ids

## conflicts
Standard teaching lists the vestibulospinal tracts (to the spinal cord, for antigravity extensor tone) among the vestibular nuclei's best-established efferent projections, alongside the cerebellum, reticular formation, oculomotor nuclei (via the MLF) and thalamus/cortex — the basal ganglia are not among the vestibular nuclei's classically described direct efferent targets. This source's printed key instead credits 'spinal cord' as the EXCEPT (non-projection) answer, which conflicts with the well-established vestibulospinal tract; 'basal ganglia' would be the more physiologically expected exception. Recorded as printed rather than silently corrected.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-F2418784E6F803

## label
Motor control of the head muscles localises to the lower part of the precentral gyrus

## canonical_key
motorhomunculus.headmuscles.lowerprecentralgyrus

## definition
The primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus, Brodmann area 4) is organised somatotopically as an inverted homunculus: the lower limb is represented in the most superior/medial part (extending onto the paracentral lobule), the trunk and upper limb in the middle convexity, and the head and face muscles in the most inferior part of the gyrus, near the lateral (Sylvian) fissure.

## explicit_objective
Localise motor control of the head muscles to the lower part of the precentral gyrus (primary motor cortex).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-95C470949A9B1B

## label
The Golgi tendon organ detects muscle tension, not length

## canonical_key
golgitendonorgan.function.detectstension

## definition
The Golgi tendon organ (GTO) is a proprioceptor located in series with extrafusal muscle fibres at the musculotendinous junction, sensitive to muscle tension (force), whether generated actively (contraction) or passively (stretch). Its Ib afferents drive the inverse myotatic (autogenic inhibition) reflex, protectively relaxing the muscle when tension becomes excessive, in contrast to the muscle spindle, which is sensitive to muscle length and lies in parallel with extrafusal fibres.

## explicit_objective
State that the Golgi tendon organ detects muscle tension (not length), distinguishing it from the muscle spindle.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-3C102BBD25467C

## label
The cerebral aqueduct (aqueduct of Sylvius) connects the third and fourth ventricles

## canonical_key
ventricularsystem.thirdfourth.aqueductofsylvius

## definition
The cerebral aqueduct (aqueduct of Sylvius) runs through the midbrain tegmentum and is the sole channel connecting the third ventricle (rostral) to the fourth ventricle (caudal), completing the CSF pathway from the lateral ventricles (via the foramina of Monro) through the third ventricle, aqueduct, and fourth ventricle before CSF exits into the subarachnoid space via the foramina of Luschka and Magendie.

## explicit_objective
Identify the cerebral aqueduct (aqueduct of Sylvius) as the connection between the third and fourth ventricles.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E5CCE4F988C6F4

## label
The Edinger-Westphal nucleus is classified as general visceral efferent (GVE)

## canonical_key
edingerwestphalnucleus.classification.gve

## definition
The Edinger-Westphal nucleus is the accessory parasympathetic nucleus of the oculomotor nerve complex, supplying preganglionic parasympathetic fibres to the ciliary ganglion for pupillary constriction (light reflex) and lens accommodation (near reflex). As autonomic (visceral) motor output, it is functionally classified in the general visceral efferent (GVE) column of the brainstem cranial nerve nuclei.

## explicit_objective
Classify the Edinger-Westphal nucleus as general visceral efferent (GVE).

# Item

## id
CON-FND-59964A92617288

## label
The malleus derives from the first, not second, branchial arch cartilage

## canonical_key
secondbranchialarch.mesoderm.derivatives

## definition
The second (hyoid) branchial arch cartilage (Reichert's cartilage) gives rise to the stapes, the styloid process, the stylohyoid ligament, and the lesser cornu and upper body of the hyoid bone. The malleus and incus, the other two auditory ossicles, instead derive from the first (mandibular) branchial arch cartilage (Meckel's cartilage), distinguishing them from the second arch's stapes.

## explicit_objective
Identify the malleus as a first-arch (not second-arch) auditory ossicle derivative.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-694D25887EC8D1

## label
Bipolar cells are a retinal cell type, not a component of the iris

## canonical_key
iris.histology.components

## definition
The iris is composed of a pigmented posterior epithelium and anterior stromal melanocytes, a vascularised stroma, and two smooth muscle layers (sphincter and dilator pupillae). Bipolar cells, second-order neurons of the neural retina that relay photoreceptor input to ganglion cells, are a retinal (not iridial) histological component and so are not found in the iris.

## explicit_objective
Identify bipolar cells as a retinal, not iridial, cell type when listing the iris's histological components.

definition

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E87D435671B16B

## label
Second (slow) pain is carried by C fibers

## canonical_key
painclassification.secondpain.cfibers

## definition
Pain is classically divided into first (fast) pain, sharp and well-localised, carried by myelinated A-delta fibres, and second (slow) pain, dull, burning, diffuse and poorly-localised, carried by small unmyelinated C fibres and following first pain after a short delay.

## explicit_objective
Pair second (slow) pain correctly with its C-fibre afferent, distinguishing it from first (fast, A-delta) pain.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4C6A3EC37DF6F0

## label
Motor control of the lower limb localises to the paracentral lobule

## canonical_key
motorhomunculus.lowerlimb.paracentrallobule

## definition
The lower limb occupies the most superior part of the motor homunculus on the precentral gyrus, and this representation extends over the hemisphere's superior convexity onto its medial surface, into the paracentral lobule (the medial continuation of the pre- and postcentral gyri) — explaining why parasagittal (falx) lesions classically produce contralateral leg weakness out of proportion to arm weakness.

## explicit_objective
Localise motor control of the lower limb to the paracentral lobule on the medial hemispheric surface.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-6E9E61AC70F468

## label
The lentiform nucleus (putamen + globus pallidus) is part of the basal ganglia

## canonical_key
lentiformnucleus.classification.basalganglia

## definition
The lentiform (lenticular) nucleus is a lens-shaped subcortical mass comprising the putamen laterally and the globus pallidus medially. Together with the caudate nucleus, which forms the striatum with the putamen, it constitutes the basal ganglia, the group of subcortical nuclei involved in modulating voluntary movement.

## explicit_objective
Classify the lentiform nucleus as part of the basal ganglia.

# Item

## id
CON-AND-F8F7DEF15A02C8

## label
The common carotid artery bifurcates within the carotid triangle

## canonical_key
commoncarotidartery.bifurcation.carotidtriangle

## definition
The carotid triangle of the neck, bounded by the posterior belly of digastric, the superior belly of omohyoid, and the anterior border of sternocleidomastoid, contains the bifurcation of the common carotid artery into its internal and external carotid branches (typically at the C4 level), alongside the internal jugular vein and vagus nerve.

## explicit_objective
Locate the common carotid artery's bifurcation within the carotid triangle of the neck.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-0EA6DBB28804E7

## label
Parkinsonism's resting tremor is hyperkinetic, not one of its hypokinetic features

## canonical_key
parkinsonism.hypokineticfeatures.excludestremor

## definition
Parkinsonism's clinical features split into hypokinetic (reduced-movement) manifestations — bradykinesia, hypokinetic dysarthria, reduced blinking/facial expression, shuffling festinating gait, and rigidity — and its single hyperkinetic (excess-movement) feature, the classic resting (static, 'pill-rolling') tremor, which arises from disinhibited thalamocortical oscillatory activity rather than from poverty of movement.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish Parkinsonism's hyperkinetic resting tremor from its otherwise hypokinetic clinical features.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-990071F32B51FF

## label
Loss of the cerebellum's timing function produces incoordination of movement

## canonical_key
cerebellum.timingfunction.incoordination

## definition
The cerebellum's role in timing and sequencing the onset, rate and termination of successive muscle contractions across joints is essential for smooth, coordinated movement; when this timing function is lost (cerebellar disease), the result is incoordination of movement — ataxia, dyssynergia and dysmetria — distinguishing cerebellar signs from the tremor and rigidity of basal ganglia disease.

## explicit_objective
State that loss of the cerebellum's movement-timing function results in incoordination (ataxia), not tremor or rigidity.

definition

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-C5F755F7EDFD2D

## label
The basal ganglia's indirect pathway suppresses inappropriate cortical motor programs

## canonical_key
basalganglia.indirectpathway.suppression

## definition
The basal ganglia's direct pathway disinhibits the thalamus to facilitate a selected, intended movement, while the indirect pathway increases inhibitory basal ganglia output onto the thalamus, net-suppressing thalamocortical drive to competing or inappropriate motor programs — allowing the intended movement selected by the direct pathway to proceed without interference from unwanted alternatives.

## explicit_objective
State that the basal ganglia's indirect pathway suppresses inappropriate/competing cortical motor programs.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-A1E3378A44B644

## label
Two-point tactile discrimination depends on the minimum distinguishable inter-stimulus distance

## canonical_key
tactilediscrimination.twopointthreshold

## definition
Tactile (two-point) discrimination is the ability to perceive two simultaneously applied touch stimuli as separate, and it is quantified as the minimum distance between the two points at which they can still be distinguished as two rather than one. This threshold varies inversely with receptor density and directly with receptive field size, being much finer (a few millimetres) on the fingertips and lips than on the back (several centimetres).

## explicit_objective
Define tactile (two-point) discrimination as dependent on the minimum distinguishable distance between two skin stimuli.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-005896A728FF6A

## label
Acetylcholine is removed from the synapse by hydrolysis (acetylcholinesterase)

## canonical_key
acetylcholine.removal.hydrolysis

## definition
Acetylcholine's action at the synapse (or neuromuscular junction) is terminated by rapid enzymatic hydrolysis, catalysed by acetylcholinesterase, which splits it into choline and acetate. This rapid hydrolysis allows the postsynaptic membrane to repolarize quickly and be ready to respond to the next nerve impulse.

## explicit_objective
State that acetylcholine is inactivated by hydrolysis (via acetylcholinesterase), permitting rapid repolarization.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-7455034AF85FAF

## label
The EPSP is caused by postsynaptic Na+ influx

## canonical_key
epsp.mechanism.sodiuminflux

## definition
The excitatory postsynaptic potential (EPSP) is a graded depolarization of the postsynaptic membrane produced when an excitatory neurotransmitter opens ligand-gated cation channels permeable mainly to Na+, and the resulting net Na+ influx brings the membrane closer to the threshold for firing an action potential — in contrast to the inhibitory postsynaptic potential (IPSP), a hyperpolarization typically driven by K+ or Cl- influx.

## explicit_objective
State that the EPSP is caused by postsynaptic Na+ influx, distinguishing it from the hyperpolarizing IPSP.

definition

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-BBE1D0545EA0C7

## label
Beta waves dominate the EEG in an alert, active individual

## canonical_key
eeg.alertstate.betawave

## definition
EEG rhythms correlate with the level of arousal: beta waves (>13 Hz, low amplitude, desynchronised) dominate during alert wakefulness with active mental engagement; alpha waves (8-13 Hz) dominate during relaxed wakefulness with eyes closed; theta waves (4-8 Hz) appear in drowsiness; and delta waves (<4 Hz) characterise deep slow-wave sleep.

## explicit_objective
Identify beta waves as the dominant EEG rhythm during alert, active wakefulness.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-214CE135633556

## label
The basal ganglia and cerebrocerebellum share a role in planning movement

## canonical_key
basalganglia.cerebrocerebellum.planningshared

## definition
Both the basal ganglia (via a cortico-basal ganglia-thalamocortical loop through the supplementary motor area) and the cerebrocerebellum (via a cortico-pontocerebellar-dentatothalamocortical loop through the lateral cerebellar hemispheres) participate in planning movement before it is executed, in addition to each structure's other, more specific roles in action selection and movement timing/coordination respectively.

## explicit_objective

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-514E5FC5F2B410

## label
The near reflex's efferent limb is postganglionic parasympathetic fibres from the Edinger-Westphal nucleus

## canonical_key
nearreflex.efferentpathway.parasympatheticew

## definition
The near reflex (convergence, pupillary constriction, and accommodation) is mediated by preganglionic parasympathetic fibres arising from the Edinger-Westphal nucleus, which synapse in the ciliary ganglion; the resulting postganglionic parasympathetic fibres supply the sphincter pupillae (pupillary constriction) and the ciliary muscle (lens accommodation), forming the reflex's efferent autonomic pathway.

## explicit_objective
Trace the near reflex's efferent autonomic pathway from the Edinger-Westphal nucleus through postganglionic parasympathetic fibres.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-3E780A5DBD4DED

## label
The hippocampus is the structure most implicated in long-term potentiation (LTP)

## canonical_key
hippocampus.ltp.mostimplicated

## definition
Long-term potentiation (LTP), a persistent activity-dependent strengthening of synaptic transmission considered a key cellular mechanism of memory, was first discovered in and is most extensively studied at hippocampal synapses (particularly the Schaffer collateral-CA1 pathway), making the hippocampus the structure most classically implicated in LTP and in the encoding of new declarative memories.

## explicit_objective
Identify the hippocampus as the structure most implicated in long-term potentiation (LTP).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-202C3567A294C6

## label
The endocochlear potential is about +80 mV, generated by stria vascularis K+ secretion

## canonical_key
endocochlearpotential.value.potassium

## definition
The endolymph filling the scala media (cochlear duct) has an unusually high K+ and low Na+ concentration, actively maintained by the stria vascularis, which generates a distinctively positive endocochlear potential of about +80 mV relative to perilymph. This large positive potential is the driving force for the mechanotransduction current that enters hair cells when their stereocilia are deflected, and is essential for normal hearing.

## explicit_objective
State the value (~+80 mV) and K+-dependent origin of the endocochlear potential.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-475F4691011072

## label
The sphenoidal air sinus lies medial to the cavernous sinus

## canonical_key
cavernoussinus.relations.medialsphenoidsinus

## definition
The cavernous sinus is a paired venous space flanking the body of the sphenoid bone, which houses the sphenoidal air sinus; the sphenoidal air sinus therefore lies medial to each cavernous sinus, with the pituitary fossa and its diaphragma sellae superior/between them — a relationship exploited surgically in the transsphenoidal approach to the pituitary.

## explicit_objective
Identify the sphenoidal air sinus as the structure lying medial to the cavernous sinus.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-9F27F5CF195109

## label
The stretch reflex's afferent limb originates from Ia spindle afferents, not alpha motor neurons

## canonical_key
stretchreflex.afferentlimb.iafibersnotalpha

## definition
The stretch (myotatic) reflex's afferent limb originates from the muscle spindle's primary (Ia) sensory afferents, which synapse directly (monosynaptically) on alpha motor neurons in the spinal cord; the alpha motor neurons themselves form the reflex's EFFERENT limb, innervating the extrafusal muscle fibres, and are not the origin of its afferent limb.

## explicit_objective
Correctly identify the muscle spindle's Ia afferents, not alpha motor neurons, as the origin of the stretch reflex's afferent limb.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-21518A0E0AF8EF

## label
Purkinje cell axons are the cerebellar cortex's sole output, terminating on the deep cerebellar nuclei

## canonical_key
purkinjecell.axontarget.deepcerebellarnuclei

## definition
Purkinje cells, large flask-shaped neurons whose cell bodies form the single-cell-thick Purkinje layer of the cerebellar cortex, are the cerebellar cortex's sole output neurons; their axons project out of the cortex to inhibit (via GABA) the deep cerebellar nuclei, the exclusive route by which cerebellar cortical processing reaches the rest of the motor system.

## explicit_objective
State that Purkinje cell axons terminate on (and inhibit) the deep cerebellar nuclei, the cerebellar cortex's sole output route.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-DB7DF3FAAE521D

## label
Pain sensation is inhibited by stimulation of opioid receptors

## canonical_key
paininhibition.opioidreceptors

## definition
The endogenous descending analgesic system, originating in the periaqueductal grey and relaying through raphe nuclei to the spinal dorsal horn, inhibits pain transmission partly via opioid receptors — both centrally, gating nociceptive input at its first synapse, and via circulating endogenous opioids (endorphins, enkephalins) — the same receptor system exploited by exogenous opioid analgesic drugs.

## explicit_objective
State that pain sensation is inhibited by stimulation of opioid receptors, part of the descending analgesic system.

# Item

## id
CON-AND-6F809D1372F6FA

## label
The incus is one of the three auditory ossicles within the middle ear

## canonical_key
middleear.components.incus

## definition
The middle ear (tympanic cavity) contains the chain of three auditory ossicles — malleus, incus, stapes — of which the incus is the middle bone, transmitting vibration from the tympanic-membrane-attached malleus to the oval-window-attached stapes, distinguishing the middle ear from the external ear (auricle, external canal) and inner ear (cochlea, organ of Corti, scalae).

## explicit_objective
Identify the incus as a component of the middle ear, distinct from external- and inner-ear structures.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-332771849CBBC6

## label
The stretch reflex is the monosynaptic spinal reflex

## canonical_key
stretchreflex.classification.monosynaptic

## definition
The stretch (myotatic) reflex is the classic monosynaptic spinal reflex: the muscle spindle's Ia afferent fibre synapses directly onto the alpha motor neuron in the spinal cord with no interposed interneuron, distinguishing it from the polysynaptic flexion (withdrawal) reflex and the disynaptic Golgi tendon (inverse myotatic) reflex.

## explicit_objective
Identify the stretch reflex as the monosynaptic spinal reflex, distinct from the polysynaptic withdrawal and Golgi tendon reflexes.

# Item

## id
CON-AND-80E0232928E2F3

## label
The jugulodigastric lymph node belongs to the deep vertical (deep cervical) chain

## canonical_key
jugulodigastriclymphnodes.classification.deepvertical

## definition
Cervical lymph nodes are organised into horizontal (superficial and deep) groups ringing the junction of head and neck, and vertical (superficial and deep) chains running along the neck. The jugulodigastric node, situated where the posterior belly of digastric crosses the internal jugular vein, belongs to the deep vertical (deep cervical) chain and is a classic site of metastasis from tonsillar and tongue-base malignancy.

## explicit_objective
Classify the jugulodigastric lymph node as a member of the deep vertical (deep cervical) chain.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-5BC9E1060D7B93

## label
Severe hypoglycemia (~40 mg/dl) provokes convulsions via neuroglycopenia

## canonical_key
hypoglycemia.convulsionthreshold.40mgdl

## definition
Because the brain relies almost exclusively on glucose for energy and has minimal glycogen reserve, severe hypoglycemia (blood glucose falling to roughly 40 mg/dl or below) produces marked neuroglycopenia, which can progress through confusion to seizures (convulsions) and, if uncorrected, coma — distinct from the milder autonomic (adrenergic) warning symptoms that appear at higher hypoglycemic levels (around 60-70 mg/dl).

## explicit_objective
State that convulsions occur at severely low blood glucose levels (approximately 40 mg/dl), due to neuroglycopenia.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-92AC1020883A5F

## label
Spasticity is an upper, not lower, motor neuron lesion feature

## canonical_key
lowermotorneuronlesion.features.excludesspasticity

## definition
Lower motor neuron lesion (LMNL) produces hypotonia (flaccidity), absent/reduced deep tendon reflexes, and marked muscle atrophy from loss of trophic innervation, together with fasciculations — the opposite pattern to upper motor neuron lesion, which produces spasticity (increased tone) from loss of descending inhibitory control, hyperreflexia, and only disuse (not denervation) atrophy.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish LMNL's hypotonic features from upper motor neuron lesion's spasticity.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-464CD5EB3C1859

## label
In the horizontal semicircular canal, the crista ampullaris's kinocilium lies toward the utricle

## canonical_key
cristaampullaris.horizontalcanal.kinociliumtowardutricle

## definition
Each vestibular hair cell of a semicircular canal's crista ampullaris has a bundle of stereocilia plus one kinocilium; deflection of the stereocilia toward the kinocilium depolarizes the cell (excitatory), while deflection away hyperpolarizes it. In the horizontal semicircular canal specifically, the kinocilium is positioned toward the utricle, so ampullopetal (utricle-directed) endolymph flow is the excitatory direction — the reverse orientation to the vertical (anterior and posterior) canals, where ampullofugal flow excites instead.

## explicit_objective
State that the horizontal semicircular canal's crista ampullaris has its kinocilium positioned toward the utricle.

Crista ampullaris, in the horizontal semicircular canal (SCC) a. Contains a single stereocilium b. Has multiple kinocilla. c. Kinocilium lies towards the utricle d. Depolarizes with the stereocilia deviated away from the kinocilium Key: C.
